An active 2026 research direction.","position":9,"markdown":"Technology · Spring 2026\n\n# _Mixture of Experts_ explained.\n\nA 2026 field guide to Mixture of Experts: how it works, why GPT-4, DeepSeek, Llama 4, Qwen3, Mistral Large 3 and Kimi K2 all rely on it, plus Super Experts, routing strategies, and the systems trade-offs MoE forces on production teams.\n\n**Miguel Neves**May 3, 202614 min read3,073 wordsFiled under **Technology**\n\nScroll\n\nFrontispiece\n\nDense models activate every parameter.MoE picks a few specialists per token —trillion-parameter capacityat the compute cost of something much smaller.The default architecture of frontier AI in 2026.\n\nInside this dispatch\n\n1. [01Foundations · what MoE is and why it works](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#foundations-what-moe-is-and-why-it-works)\n2. [02From GPT-4 to Mixtral](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#from-gpt-4-to-mixtral)\n3. [03The 2026 MoE landscape](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#the-2026-moe-landscape)\n4. [04Inside the model · routing, experts, training](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#inside-the-model-routing-experts-training)\n5. [052026 research findings](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#2026-research-findings)\n6. [06Inference, deployment, and systems](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#inference-deployment-and-systems)\n7. [07Beyond LLMs](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#beyond-llms)\n8. [08Where MoE is going](https://tensorops.ai/blog/what-is-mixture-of-experts-llm#where-moe-is-going)\n\n![LLM Mixture of Experts](https://tensorops.ai/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Ftensorops.ai%2Fapi%2Fmedia%2Ffile%2Fllm_moe.png%3F2025-08-24&w=3840&q=100)\n\nLLM Mixture of Experts\n\n_TensorOps Technology · Spring 2026 · A field guide for architects, engineers, and AI thought leaders._\n\nMixture of Experts has moved from an experimental scaling trick to the default architecture behind frontier AI. GPT-4's rumored sparse design was the early signal. Today, DeepSeek-V3/R1 and V4, Qwen3-235B-A22B, the Llama 4 family, Mistral Large 3, Kimi K2, Grok-1/2, and GPT-5/GPT-OSS all rely on it. Modern AI faces two linked challenges — exploding compute requirements and the difficulty of fitting one monolithic model to increasingly diverse data — and MoE has emerged as the dominant answer to both. The core idea is simple: MoE lets models grow dramatically larger without activating every parameter for every token. It is how the industry now builds larger, more capable systems while keeping inference cost, latency, and training compute within reach.\n\n## Foundations · what MoE is and why it works\n\n### What is Mixture of Experts?\n\nMoE is an AI architecture where a set of specialized sub-networks (\"experts\") sit inside a larger model, and a lightweight gating mechanism decides which experts process each input token. It's a divide-and-conquer strategy that optimizes for both performance and efficiency. Instead of using the whole model every time, only a small subset activates per token — letting a model contain hundreds of billions or trillions of parameters while using a fraction of them for any given input.\n\n### How does it differ from traditional ensembles?\n\nTraditional ML ensembles like boosting and bagging combine models statically. MoE is dynamic and internal to the model. For each token, a router picks the most relevant experts in real time. The result feels less like a committee vote and more like a coordinated team of specialists working inside a single neural architecture, with routing decisions baked into inference itself.\n\n### What does \"expert\" really mean here?\n\nEach expert naturally develops proficiency in different regions of a high-dimensional embedding space during training. These aren't experts in the human sense — categorizing them as \"the math expert\" or \"the code expert\" is a useful conceptual shorthand, but their actual specialization is statistical and emergent. The router learns which expert is best for which token representation, and refines those decisions over training.\n\n### Why does MoE matter for modern LLMs?\n\nDense models activate all parameters for every token, which makes scaling expensive. MoE breaks the link between total model capacity and active compute. A 671B-parameter MoE may activate only ~37B per token, giving it enormous representational capacity at the cost profile of a much smaller dense model. This decoupling is the single most important shift in frontier-model design over the past two years.\n\n### What problem does MoE actually solve?\n\nModern LLMs have to handle code, math, conversation, multilingual reasoning, vision, retrieval, and agentic workflows — each with different demands. A single dense feed-forward path forces all of this through the same parameters, creating interference. MoE lets the model specialize internally, routing different inputs through different expert pathways without ballooning per-token compute.\n\n### What happens inside an MoE layer?\n\nIn most LLMs, MoE replaces some dense feed-forward (FFN) layers with expert-based FFN blocks. The router scores available experts for a token and dispatches it to the top-ranked one or two. Classic routing uses Top-K softmax gating: `G(x) = softmax(TopK(W_g · x + noise, K))`. Mixtral used Top-2; newer systems lean toward finer-grained experts, shared experts, and sigmoid gating to reduce winner-take-all dynamics.\n\nAnatomy of an MoE layer — only top-K of N experts activate per tokenFig. 01 · Inside an MoE layerDense layers run every parameter for every token. MoE layers route each token to only K of N experts.Dense FFNall params active · every tokenTokenFFN100% of weightsOutputcost ∝ total paramsMoE FFNrouter picks top-K of N experts per tokenTokenRouterG(x) · softmax · TopKE1E2E3E4E5E6E7E8ΣOutputcost ∝ active paramsMixtral picks 2 of 8 experts per token · DeepSeek-V3 picks 8 of 256 routed experts plus 1 always-on shared expert\n\n## From GPT-4 to Mixtral\n\n### Why did GPT-4 become associated with MoE?\n\nOn June 20, 2023, George Hotz revealed that GPT-4 was rumored to be eight smaller models of ~220B parameters each — later echoed by Soumith Chintala. That gives a headline figure of ~1.76T parameters, but the math isn't that clean: in MoE transformers, typically only the FFN layers are replicated per expert while attention layers are shared. The _true_ total for GPT-4 likely sits between **1.2T and 1.76T**. OpenAI never confirmed the architecture, but inference-cost patterns and reverse-engineering made sparse activation the credible explanation.\n\n### How should we read the \"lazy GPT-4\" debate?\n\nSome users described later GPT-4 variants as less thorough or \"lazier\" than earlier releases. Three plausible causes, all consistent with MoE-based serving:\n\n- **Cost reduction:** Every expert must be loaded into VRAM, so reducing the number or size of experts used per query has a big impact on cost — and on quality.\n- **Aggressive RLHF:** Continuous reinforcement learning with human feedback makes models safer and more useful for specific products, but can flatten creativity.\n- **Distillation and quantization:** Compressing the MoE into a smaller dense model or reducing weight precision cuts costs further.\n\nMoE isn't inherently weaker; it gives operators more levers to trade cost, latency, and quality.\n\n### What is OpenAI doing with MoE now?\n\nGPT-5 and the GPT-OSS open-weight release (2025) use multimodal MoE with experts specialized across coding, math, conversation, and vision. The lineage is now openly sparse, even if exact parameter counts remain undisclosed.\n\n### What made Mixtral the turning point?\n\nMixtral 8x7B (2023) proved that open-weight MoE could be practical and competitive. It's a sparse mixture-of-experts (SMoE) decoder-only model under Apache 2.0:\n\n- **Total / active parameters:** 46.7B total, ~12.9B active.\n- **Expert mechanism:** Each FFN block picks from eight distinct expert groups.\n- **Token routing:** A router selects two experts per token (Top-2).\n- **Additive combination:** The outputs of the two chosen experts are combined additively, blending their specialized knowledge.\n- **Efficiency:** Operates at the speed and cost of a 12.9B model despite a 46.7B total footprint, ~6× faster than Llama 2 70B at higher quality.\n\nMixtral 8x22B followed with 141B total / 39B active, pushing stronger results in coding and math.\n\n### What changed after Mixtral?\n\nThe industry shifted from small expert pools to much finer-grained systems — 128, 256, or more routed experts, often combined with always-on shared experts. This improves specialization and routing flexibility while reducing the risk that a few coarse experts become overloaded or poorly differentiated.\n\n## The 2026 MoE landscape\n\n### What are the leading open MoE models in 2026?\n\nThe 2025–2026 generation pushed MoE into much larger deployments:\n\n- **DeepSeek-V3 / R1 —** ~671B total / ~37B active. 256 routed experts plus 1 shared expert, with auxiliary-loss-free load balancing.\n- **DeepSeek-V4 (April 2026) —** 1.6T total / 49B active in the Pro variant; a Flash variant at 284B total / 13B active. Native 1M-token context, trained on >32T tokens.\n- **Qwen3-235B-A22B —** 235B total / ~22B active, activating 8 experts per token from a pool of 128. Strong multilingual and agentic performance.\n- **Llama 4 Maverick —** Meta's first major MoE flagship, ~400B total / ~17B active, 128 routed plus 1 shared expert per MoE layer, interleaved dense/MoE layers, native multimodality.\n- **Mistral Large 3 —** 675B total / 41B active, 256K context, top open-source coding performer on LM Arena, deployable on a single 8-GPU node.\n- **Kimi K2 —** ~1T total / ~32B active, natively multimodal.\n- **Grok-1 / Grok-2 —** xAI's open MoE releases (314B total, ~70–80B active for Grok-1) extended the open ecosystem beyond DeepSeek, Qwen, Mistral, and Meta.\n\nTotal vs active parameters across leading 2026 MoE modelsFig. 02 · Total vs active parametersMoE decouples model capacity from per-token compute. Active parameters (accent) are a small fraction of total (ink).0250B500B750B1000B1250B1500B1700BParametersDeepSeek-V4 Pro1600B total49B activeKimi K21000B total32B activeMistral Large 3675B total41B activeDeepSeek-V3 / R1671B total37B activeLlama 4 Maverick400B total17B activeQwen3-235B-A22B235B total22B activeLlama 4 Scout109B total17B activetotal parametersactive per tokenDeepSeek-V4 Pro carries 33× more parameters than it activates per token · this is the gap MoE was built to exploit\n\n### What about the full Llama 4 family?\n\nLlama 4 expanded into a family rather than a single release. **Scout** is the smaller variant (109B total / 17B active, with up to 10M-token context in some configs, runnable on a single H100). **Maverick** is the larger flagship at ~400B / 17B active. **Behemoth** is a research-scale system with significantly larger active capacity — some reports cite ~288B active. The design reflects a broader trend: MoE is now a systems architecture, not just a model-layer trick.\n\n### Why are shared experts important?\n\nShared experts process every token and provide stability, common knowledge, and a baseline representation that all routed paths build on. DeepSeek and Llama 4 both use them. This reduces fragmentation: routed experts can specialize aggressively while shared experts preserve global continuity across tasks, languages, modalities, and reasoning patterns.\n\nShared + routed expert architecture (DeepSeek, Llama 4)Fig. 04 · Shared + routed expertsAlways-on shared expert preserves global continuity; routed experts specialize and fire only when picked.TokenRouterscores routed poolalways onShared expertstability · global knowledgetop-KE1E2E3E4E5E6E7E8E9E10E11E12E13E14E15E16Routed pool · N expertsΣOutputDeepSeek-V3 · 1 shared expert + 256 routed (top-8) · Llama 4 Maverick · 1 shared + 128 routedShared experts absorb common patterns so routed experts can specialize without fragmenting basic capability\n\n## Inside the model · routing, experts, training\n\n### What is the router actually learning?\n\nThe router learns which expert pathways are useful for each token. During training, different experts naturally become better suited to different regions of the embedding space. But specialization is rarely as clean as \"one expert for code, one for math.\" Experts often specialize in syntax, entities, languages, token patterns, reasoning modes, or hidden internal features that don't map neatly to task labels — specialization is more emergent than designed.\n\n### What gating designs are in use?\n\nThe router is the conductor of the orchestra, and its design is critical:\n\n- **Linear (softmax) gating —** A linear layer plus softmax produces expert probabilities, with Top-K selecting the winners. Noise added during training encourages exploration. Used by Mixtral and most production MoEs.\n- **Cosine routers —** Compute cosine similarity between input and a learned embedding for each expert. Better at handling cross-domain data and improving generalization.\n- **Sigmoid gating —** Reduces winner-take-all competition between experts.\n- **Routing-free designs —** Experts self-activate via learned mechanisms, eliminating the explicit router bottleneck. An active 2026 research direction.\n\n### What kinds of experts exist beyond FFN?\n\nThree families matter:\n\n- **FFN experts —** The dominant pattern in LLMs (GPT-4, Mixtral, DeepSeek, Llama 4). FFN layers carry a large fraction of transformer compute and show natural specialization tendencies.\n- **Attention experts (Mixture-of-Attention, MoA) —** Each expert is a distinct attention head; the router picks the most relevant heads per token.\n- **CNN experts —** In computer vision, each expert is a set of convolutional layers tuned for different visual features or image types.\n\n### What are the main routing strategies?\n\nToken-level routing remains the standard for LLMs — each token independently routed based on its hidden representation. **Task-level routing** sends all tokens for a given task (translation vs. summarization) to dedicated experts, minimizing interference. **Modality-level routing** sends text tokens to text experts and image patches to vision experts in multimodal systems — increasingly common as native multimodality becomes the norm.\n\n### How is MoE training stabilized?\n\nThe central challenge is **load balancing**. If the router sends most tokens to a few favorite experts, the model collapses, wasting capacity. Classic MoE adds an **auxiliary loss** that penalizes unbalanced routing decisions. DeepSeek-style designs now lead with **auxiliary-loss-free** load balancing, supplemented by orthogonality losses, variance objectives, and router regularization. The goal is genuine specialization rather than artificial uniformity. Recent work also addresses gradient noise, RL instability specific to sparse models, and expert collapse.\n\n## 2026 research findings\n\n### What are Super Experts, and why are they the headline 2026 finding?\n\nICLR 2026 papers (and arXiv 2507.23279) identified a tiny subset of experts — often just 3–10 out of thousands — that dominate extreme activation outliers in the down\\_proj layer and create massive hidden-state activations between decoder layers. Pruning just **3 of 6,144 experts in Qwen3-30B-A3B** caused catastrophic collapse: repetitive, uninformative outputs and major drops in math and reasoning. Super Experts are model-specific, data-agnostic, and unaffected by post-training.\n\n### Why do Super Experts matter for deployment?\n\nThey reshape how teams think about compression, pruning, and quantization. If a handful of experts carry disproportionate responsibility, naive pruning destroys quality. They also offer a window into _why_ MoE works: Super Experts appear connected to hidden-state outliers and attention-sink behavior, suggesting they stabilize information flow across layers. Compression strategies built around Super Expert preservation are now an active research area.\n\n### What is MoE entanglement?\n\nICLR 2026 cross-layer routing studies show that MoE layers are not independent. Activations from one MoE layer strongly predict which experts fire in subsequent layers, and MoE outputs often dominate routing decisions more than attention layers do. This cross-layer dependency — MoE entanglement — means routing forms a dynamic pathway through the network, with expert outputs shaping future selection more strongly than earlier work assumed.\n\n### Does MoE reduce polysemanticity?\n\nThere is growing evidence that sparse routing reduces some forms of superposition compared with dense models of similar active size. Because different token patterns route through different experts, features become less entangled. Mechanistic studies on OLMoE and Qwen3 variants have automatically labeled experts as detectors for subword families, proper names, locations, languages, and entity types — though the mapping remains imperfect.\n\n## Inference, deployment, and systems\n\n### Why is MoE faster at inference?\n\nOnly active parameters matter for compute. A model with 671B total but 37B active has per-token compute resembling a much smaller dense model. In production this means better throughput, lower marginal cost, or higher quality at the same serving budget — typically 5–10× better cost-performance than equivalent dense models, and meaningful speedups over comparable dense systems (Mixtral was ~6× faster than Llama 2 70B at higher quality).\n\nSchematic capability frontier — MoE Pareto curve sits above dense at the same active computeFig. 05 · Capability per active parameterSchematic. At equal active compute per token, MoE models sit on a higher capability frontier than dense models — the gap is the value MoE captures.020B40B60B80B100B120BActive parameters per tokenRelative capabilityMixtral 8x7BLlama 4 ScoutQwen3-A22BDeepSeek-V3Mistral L3Llama 3 8BLlama 3 70BDense frontierMoE frontiercapability liftSchematic — vertical scale is illustrative. Active-parameter counts are publicly disclosed by model authors.\n\n### Why is MoE still hard to deploy?\n\nThe full model has to live somewhere. Even if only a few experts activate per token, the serving stack must load, shard, offload, or retrieve the full expert set efficiently. That creates VRAM, bandwidth, and orchestration challenges. Even Mixtral required at least 30 GB of VRAM and high-end GPUs (A100, A6000, H100); frontier-scale MoEs need clusters or aggressive quantization. Production MoE serving depends heavily on expert parallelism, quantization (FP8, INT4, 4-bit with QLoRA-style techniques for attention layers), fast routing kernels, communication scheduling, and hardware-aware placement.\n\nThe deployment paradox — small per-token compute, large memory footprintFig. 06 · The deployment paradoxPer-token compute is small (active params). Memory is huge (every expert must be loaded somewhere). Both must be planned for.0250 GB500 GB750 GB1.0 TB1.2 TB1.5 TB1.7 TBVRAM (≈ FP8 weights) · Active params (B)active params per token (GB equiv.)VRAM to serve full model (FP8)Mixtral 8x7B47 GB12.9B activeLlama 4 Scout109 GB17B activeQwen3-A22B235 GB22B activeDeepSeek-V3671 GB37B activeMistral L3675 GB41B activeDeepSeek-V4 Pro1600 GB49B activeFP8 weight footprint ≈ 1 byte per parameter. Add KV cache, activation buffers, and expert-parallel comms overhead in production.Llama 4 Scout fits a single H100 (80 GB) only with quantization · DeepSeek-V4 Pro needs an 8-GPU node minimum, even at FP8.\n\n### What hardware and systems advances are changing MoE?\n\nThe 2026 acceleration surveys (arXiv 2503.07137 updated; \"A Survey on Accelerated Technologies for MoE\") cover hybrid parallel computing, fine-grained memory management, communication scheduling, ML-guided load balancing, cross-layer optimization, and hardware-software co-design. **NVIDIA Blackwell NVL72** reportedly delivers ~10× inference gains on DeepSeek-R1, Kimi K2, and Mistral Large 3. Wide-expert parallelism, FP8 and INT4 quantization, and SSD expert offloading for consumer hardware are all moving from research into production.\n\n### Can MoE run on smaller infrastructure?\n\nIncreasingly, yes. **DeepSeek-V3.2 Speciale** fits on 8×H100 with FP8. **Llama 4 Scout** runs on a single H100. **Mistral Large 3** deploys on a single 8-GPU node. Heavy quantization, expert offloading, and specialized inference engines have made smaller deployments realistic, but frontier-scale MoEs still require serious memory capacity. The defining tradeoff remains the gap between active compute (small) and memory footprint (huge).\n\n### How does MoE affect fine-tuning?\n\nEasier than in 2024 but still more complex than dense fine-tuning. Teams must account for routing behavior, expert balance, memory layout, and whether adapters target shared layers, routed experts, or both. **Unsloth** offers 12–30× faster MoE training; Hugging Face expert backends and native vLLM support have made MoE first-class citizens. For narrow enterprise tasks with constrained infrastructure, dense models may still be simpler.\n\n## Beyond LLMs\n\n### Where is MoE used beyond LLMs?\n\nThe technique generalizes well across AI:\n\n- **Computer vision —** V-MoE scaled vision transformers to 15B parameters with experts specializing in different visual features (one for fur, another for faces). Swin-MoE extends this to hierarchical vision transformers.\n- **Reinforcement learning —** Different experts learn distinct policies or skills (walking vs. jumping), with the gating network choosing based on the agent's state or goal.\n- **Multimodal models —** MoE-LLaVA, Qwen3-VL, and others coordinate text, image, video, code, and tool-use pathways through specialized experts.\n- **Agentic systems —** Task-specific experts handle planning, retrieval, and tool calls without cross-interference.\n\nThe core advantage is consistent: reduce interference by letting different experts handle different data regimes.\n\n### How does MoE relate to multimodality?\n\nNative multimodality is now standard across flagship MoEs — Qwen3, Llama 4, Mistral Large 3, Kimi K2, and DeepSeek-V4, not just the GPT-5 lineage. Instead of bolting vision onto a text model, newer systems route different modalities through specialized expert pathways. This matters for enterprise architectures: document understanding, code agents, visual inspection, long-context retrieval, and workflow automation increasingly need models that coordinate multiple data types without collapsing them into one overloaded representation.\n\n### How does MoE fit into the post-Transformer discussion?\n\nMoE is still transformer-based but belongs to the broader 2026 conversation about changing scaling laws — alongside state-space models, Mamba-style architectures, retrieval, and agentic control. MoE's advantage is that it works _with_ today's transformer ecosystem. It improves scaling efficiency without requiring the industry to abandon the architecture, tooling, and hardware paths already built.\n\n## Where MoE is going\n\n### A brief evolution\n\n- **1991** — Jacobs and Hinton publish _Adaptive Mixtures of Local Experts_.\n- **2014** — MoE first applied to modern deep learning.\n- **2017** — Google's _Outrageously Large Neural Networks_ (Hinton, Shazeer, et al.) proposes MoE for large-scale models.\n- **2020** — GShard scales MoE to giant transformers for machine translation.\n- **2022** — Switch Transformers cross 1T parameters and address MoE training and fine-tuning issues.\n- **2023** — Mixtral 8x7B brings MoE to open source.\n- **2024–2025** — DeepSeek-V2/V3, Qwen3, Llama 4, GPT-OSS, Mistral Large 3 establish MoE as the standard.\n- **2026** — Focus shifts to Super Experts and interpretability, routing-free designs, multimodal MoE, and inference-time optimization.\n\nFrom Jacobs & Hinton (1991) to Super Experts (2026)Fig. 03 · A brief evolution of MoEThree decades from a regional-experts paper to the default architecture of frontier AI.1991Adaptive Mixturesof Local Experts2014MoE meetsdeep learning2017Outrageously LargeNeural Networks2020GShardscales to T-params2022Switch Transformercrosses 1T2023Mixtral 8x7Bopen-weight MoE2024–25DeepSeek · QwenLlama 4 · GPT-OSS2026Super Experts· entanglementThe 2017 paper from Hinton, Shazeer et al. is the inflection — MoE moved from idea to scaling lever for transformers\n\n### What are the most important open-source MoE directions?\n\nThe ecosystem has exploded. Megablocks, DeepSpeed-MoE, Fairseq, OpenMoE, and Unsloth all power MoE workloads, with native support in Hugging Face Transformers and vLLM. Active research directions include:\n\n- **Distilling MoEs** into smaller dense models that retain performance.\n- **Quantization** for memory-footprint reduction (FP8, INT4).\n- **Model merging** and new ways to combine experts.\n- **Routing-free and self-activating experts.**\n- **Orthogonality losses** for genuine specialization.\n- **Compression strategies** built around Super Expert preservation.\n- **Hybrid MoE** plus retrieval or agentic control.\n- **Long-context multimodal variants.**\n- **Hardware co-design** for sparse activation patterns.\n\n### Why is MoE now the default scaling recipe?\n\nIt offers a practical answer to the frontier-model scaling problem. Labs can build models with massive total capacity while controlling per-token compute. That combination — quality, lower inference cost, specialization, and a real path to trillion-parameter systems — is hard to beat economically with dense architectures.\n\n### What should AI architects take away?\n\nMoE changes model architecture, but it also changes infrastructure architecture. The design question is no longer just \"How many parameters does the model have?\" but \"How many parameters are active, where are the experts stored, and how efficiently can they be routed?\" Production teams must think across the full stack: model design, memory layout, parallelism, quantization, routing behavior, observability, and cost controls. Sparse intelligence is as much a systems problem as a modeling breakthrough.\n\n### What is the bottom line?\n\nMoE is no longer exotic — it is the architecture that makes trillion-parameter AI practical. By activating only a fraction of parameters per token, it delivers higher capacity, stronger specialization, faster inference, and better cost-performance than dense models of equivalent total size. As of spring 2026, the vast majority of frontier open and closed models are MoE-based, and the gap between open and closed performance has never been smaller. The era of purely monolithic dense frontier models is giving way to sparse, specialized, and ruthlessly efficient AI systems. The story of MoE is still being written — but it's already the most consequential architectural shift of the decade.\n\n_TensorOps · Your partners in AI_\n\nEnd.   Set in Fraunces, Newsreader & JetBrains Mono.\n\nTensorOps · Blog · 2026\n\nWe value your privacy\n\nWe use necessary cookies to operate this site. With your permission, we also use analytics and advertising cookies to measure performance and improve our campaigns. Mixture-of-Experts","description":"# Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n### Summary\n- Routing-Free MoE consistently reduced validation perplexity (e.g., by -18.8% at scale L) and improved average RFMoE consistently achieves lower perplexity across all compute scales.](https://paper-assets.alphaxiv.org/figures-normalized/figures/2604.00801v1/x2.png)_Figure 2: Scaling laws and perplexity comparisons for different MoE variants._\n\nThe results indicated that RFMoE consistently achieves lower validation perplexity than standard MoE across all scales.","position":10,"markdown":"[Explore](https://www.alphaxiv.org/)\n\n[Researchers](https://www.alphaxiv.org/researchers)\n\n[Sign In](https://www.alphaxiv.org/signin?flow=%2Foverview%2F2604.00801v1)\n\n[MCP Server](https://www.alphaxiv.org/docs/mcp)\n\n[Compute](https://openresearch.sh/compute)\n\n[Browser Extension](https://chromewebstore.google.com/detail/alphaxiv-understand-resea/liihfcjialakefgidmaadhajjikbjjab)\n\n[Twitter](https://twitter.com/askalphaxiv)\n\n[Blog](https://www.alphaxiv.org/blog) [Send Feedback?](https://github.com/alphaxiv/feedback/issues)\n\n[Paper](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2604.00801v1) [Blog](https://www.alphaxiv.org/overview/2604.00801v1) [Autoresearch](https://www.alphaxiv.org/replicate/2604.00801v1)\n\n26\n\nen\n\nHide Tools\nCtrl + /\n\nOpen Tools\n\n# Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\n### Summary\n\nResearchers developed a Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture that eliminates all centralized routing mechanisms, resulting in improved language modeling quality and downstream task performance. This novel design allows experts to autonomously determine activation, achieving a -18.8% perplexity improvement at scale L and enhanced throughput in distributed inference environments.\n\nSummary\n\nProblem\n\nMethod\n\nResults\n\nTakeaways\n\nAbstract\n\nResearchers developed a Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture that eliminates all centralized routing mechanisms, resulting in improved language modeling quality and downstream task performance. This novel design allows experts to autonomously determine activation, achieving a -18.8% perplexity improvement at scale L and enhanced throughput in distributed inference environments.\n\n- Traditional Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures rely on centralized routers with limited capacity, leading to suboptimal expert routing and unstable training dynamics.\n- Conventional MoE designs impose rigid activation constraints like fixed TopK selection and Softmax normalization, which hinder adaptability to varying input complexities and disregard absolute expert suitability scores.\n- Existing load-balancing strategies in MoE are often inflexible, offering a choice between mutually exclusive objectives (token-balancing or expert-balancing) rather than adaptive resource allocation.\n\n- The Routing-Free MoE architecture eliminates centralized routers, TopK selection, and Softmax, allowing each expert to autonomously determine its activation based on internal confidence scores.\n- A learnable per-expert bias and a global post-activation threshold are introduced to control individual expert participation and overall sparsity.\n- A unified adaptive load-balancing framework combines token-balancing and expert-balancing objectives using a configurable interpolation parameter, with an adaptively scheduled loss coefficient to target desired computational budgets.\n\n- Routing-Free MoE consistently reduced validation perplexity (e.g., by -18.8% at scale L) and improved average performance on 9 downstream benchmarks compared to standard MoE.\n- The architecture demonstrated enhanced training stability, tolerating a wider range of learning rates, and achieved higher throughput in expert-parallel deployment scenarios.\n- The adaptive load-balancing framework effectively guided activation density to target levels, with a balanced interpolation (μ=0.5) yielding optimal perplexity and throughput.\n\n- Removing centralized routing and rigid activation constraints allows experts to specialize more effectively and leads to superior language modeling quality and downstream task performance.\n- An adaptive and unified load-balancing approach, interpolating between token and expert objectives, achieves optimal throughput and perplexity.\n- The model self-organizes into distinct per-layer activation patterns (e.g., three-stage structure), suggesting emergent functional specialization when rigid sparsity biases are lifted.\n\nStandard Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models rely on centralized routing mechanisms that introduce rigid inductive biases. We propose Routing-Free MoE which eliminates any hard-coded centralized designs including external routers, Softmax, Top-K and load balancing, instead encapsulating all activation functionalities within individual experts and directly optimized through continuous gradient flow, enabling each expert to determine its activation entirely on its own. We introduce a unified adaptive load-balancing framework to simultaneously optimize both expert-balancing and token-balancing objectives through a configurable interpolation, allowing flexible and customizable resource allocation. Extensive experiments show that Routing-Free MoE can consistently outperform baselines with better scalability and robustness. We analyze its behavior in detail and offer insights that may facilitate future MoE design ad optimization.\n\nGenerate audio summary→\n\n## Table of Contents\n\n### The Bottleneck of Centralized Routing\n\n### The Architecture of Routing-Free MoE\n\n### Internal Scoring and ReLU Gating\n\n### Global Sparsity Control\n\n### A Unified and Adaptive Load Balancing Framework\n\n### Adaptive Coefficient Schedule\n\n### Performance and Scalability\n\n### Training Robustness\n\n### Emergent Activation Patterns\n\n### Deployment Efficiency\n\n### Significance of the Work\n\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) continue to demand increasing computational resources for both training and deployment. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have become a standard approach to mitigate these costs by decoupling a model's total parameter count from its computational overhead. By activating only a small subset of \"experts\" for any given input, MoE models achieve high capacity without a linear increase in floating-point operations.\n\nDespite their success, conventional MoE designs rely on centralized routing mechanisms that introduce several structural constraints. The \"Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\" (RFMoE) architecture proposed by Liu et al. provides an alternative framework that eliminates these centralized bottlenecks, allowing individual experts to autonomously determine their activation based on internal confidence scores.\n\n![Comparison between Standard MoE and Routing-Free MoE architectures. In RFMoE, experts independently decide their activation without a centralized router.](https://paper-assets.alphaxiv.org/figures-normalized/figures/2604.00801v1/x1.png)_Figure 1: Comparison between Standard MoE and Routing-Free MoE architectures. In RFMoE, experts independently decide their activation without a centralized router._\n\n## The Bottleneck of Centralized Routing\n\nCurrent MoE implementations typically utilize an external \"router\"—a small neural network—to assign incoming tokens to specific experts. This design, while functional, suffers from three primary limitations:\n\n1. **Capacity Gap:** The router is significantly smaller than the experts it manages. This limited capacity restricts its ability to fully capture the complex specializations of each expert, often leading to suboptimal routing decisions and unstable training dynamics in early stages.\n2. **Rigid TopK Selection:** Standard MoE usually selects exactly KKK experts for every token (e.g., Top-1 or Top-2). This fixed sparsity ratio does not account for varying input complexity; some tokens might naturally require more expert knowledge than others.\n3. **Softmax Information Loss:** Routers often use a Softmax function to produce a probability distribution over experts. This operation normalizes scores such that an expert's absolute suitability is lost in favor of relative rankings. A highly qualified expert might be suppressed simply because other experts also received high scores, or a poorly suited expert might be activated because it was the \"best of a bad batch.\"\n\nThe RFMoE framework seeks to address these issues by decentralizing the selection process, removing the need for a global router, TopK selection, or Softmax normalization.\n\n## The Architecture of Routing-Free MoE\n\nThe core principle of RFMoE is \"Expert-Internal Activation.\" Instead of being assigned work by an external manager, each expert EiE\\_iEi​ independently assesses its own relevance to an input token xxx.\n\n### Internal Scoring and ReLU Gating\n\nIn RFMoE, each expert generates an internal representation via a low-rank projection Agate,iA\\_{\\\\text{gate},i}Agate,i​. The norm of this projection serves as an indicator of how well the input aligns with the expert's learned domain. To enable sparse activation, the architecture introduces a learnable, per-expert bias bib\\_ibi​. The suitability score Gi(x)G\\_i(x)Gi​(x) is calculated as:\n\nGi(x)=ReLU(∥xAgate,i∥2−bi)G\\_i(x) = \\\\text{ReLU}(\\\\\\|x A\\_{\\\\text{gate},i}\\\\\\|^2 - b\\_i)Gi​(x)=ReLU(∥xAgate,i​∥2−bi​)\n\nThis mechanism allows the expert to adjust its own activation threshold through standard backpropagation. If the weighted norm of the input falls below the bias bib\\_ibi​, the expert remains inactive (Gi(x)=0G\\_i(x) = 0Gi​(x)=0).\n\n### Global Sparsity Control\n\nWhile experts make local decisions, the system still requires a way to regulate the total computational budget. To achieve this, RFMoE introduces a global configurable hyperparameter θ\\\\thetaθ. The final binary activation decision fi(x)f\\_i(x)fi​(x) for an expert is defined as:\n\nfi(x)=1{Gi(x)−θ≥0}f\\_i(x) = \\\\mathbb{1}\\\\{G\\_i(x) - \\\\theta \\\\geq 0\\\\}fi​(x)=1{Gi​(x)−θ≥0}\n\nThis two-tier system combines local adaptivity with global control. The local biases bib\\_ibi​ allow experts to specialize during training, while the global threshold θ\\\\thetaθ provides a mechanism to adjust the overall model sparsity even after training is complete.\n\n## A Unified and Adaptive Load Balancing Framework\n\nMaintaining a balance between experts is a common challenge in MoE training. If left unchecked, \"expert collapse\" can occur, where a few experts become over-specialized and handle all tokens while others remain unused. Conversely, forcing every token to use a fixed number of experts can be inefficient.\n\nRFMoE introduces a unified auxiliary loss LLBL\\_{\\\\text{LB}}LLB​ that interpolates between two balancing objectives:\n\n- **Expert-Balancing (EB):** Ensures that across a batch of tokens, the workload is distributed evenly among all experts.\n- **Token-Balancing (TB):** Ensures that for any given token, the number of activated experts remains consistent with the desired computational budget.\n\nThese objectives are combined using a configurable interpolation parameter μ∈\\[0,1\\]\\\\mu \\\\in \\[0, 1\\]μ∈\\[0,1\\]:\n\nLLB=μLEB+(1−μ)LTBL\\_{\\\\text{LB}} = \\\\mu L\\_{\\\\text{EB}} + (1 - \\\\mu) L\\_{\\\\text{TB}}LLB​=μLEB​+(1−μ)LTB​\n\nWhen μ=1\\\\mu = 1μ=1, the model prioritizes expert balance; when μ=0\\\\mu = 0μ=0, it prioritizes token balance. The authors found that a balanced setting (μ=0.5\\\\mu = 0.5μ=0.5) generally provides the best trade-off between language modeling quality and computational throughput.\n\n### Adaptive Coefficient Schedule\n\nIn traditional MoE, the weight of the load-balancing loss λ\\\\lambdaλ is a static hyperparameter that requires careful tuning. RFMoE replaces this with an adaptive schedule. At each training step ttt, the coefficient λ\\\\lambdaλ is updated based on the difference between the current activation density ρt(E,B)\\\\rho\\_t(E,B)ρt​(E,B) and the target density ρ∞\\\\rho\\_\\\\inftyρ∞​:\n\nλt+1=λt⋅(1+η)sign(ρt(E,B)−ρ∞)\\\\lambda\\_{t+1} = \\\\lambda\\_t \\\\cdot (1 + \\\\eta)^{\\\\text{sign}(\\\\rho\\_t(E, B) - \\\\rho\\_\\\\infty)}λt+1​=λt​⋅(1+η)sign(ρt​(E,B)−ρ∞​)\n\nwhere η\\\\etaη is a small adjustment rate. This mechanism automatically increases the pressure to balance experts if the model becomes too dense and relaxes it when the target budget is met. This ensures that the load-balancing pressure is only applied when necessary, minimizing interference with the primary language modeling objective LLML\\_{\\\\text{LM}}LLM​.\n\n## Performance and Scalability\n\nThe researchers evaluated RFMoE across several model scales (S, M, and L) up to 0.8 billion parameters, comparing it against standard MoE and other recent variants like Autonomy-of-Experts (AoE).\n\n![Scaling laws and perplexity comparisons for different MoE variants. RFMoE consistently achieves lower perplexity across all compute scales.](https://paper-assets.alphaxiv.org/figures-normalized/figures/2604.00801v1/x2.png)_Figure 2: Scaling laws and perplexity comparisons for different MoE variants. RFMoE consistently achieves lower perplexity across all compute scales._\n\nThe results indicated that RFMoE consistently achieves lower validation perplexity than standard MoE across all scales. For example, at scale L, RFMoE showed an 18.8% improvement in perplexity over the baseline. Furthermore, the model demonstrated superior performance across nine zero-shot downstream tasks, including PIQA, HellaSwag, and ARC.\n\n### Training Robustness\n\nOne of the practical benefits of the routing-free approach is increased stability. Standard MoE models are notoriously sensitive to the learning rate α\\\\alphaα. High learning rates can lead to training collapse as the centralized router fails to adapt to rapidly changing expert representations. RFMoE, however, maintains stability across a much wider range of learning rates, particularly as the model scales up.\n\n![Training stability comparison. RFMoE (yellow) remains stable at higher learning rates where standard MoE (red) experiences perplexity spikes or collapse.](https://paper-assets.alphaxiv.org/figures-normalized/figures/2604.00801v1/x9.png)_Figure 3: Training stability comparison. RFMoE (yellow) remains stable at higher learning rates where standard MoE (red) experiences perplexity spikes or collapse._\n\n## Emergent Activation Patterns\n\nA notable finding in the study was the self-organization of activation patterns across different layers. In standard MoE, a fixed sparsity (e.g., Top-2) is typically enforced uniformly across every layer. In RFMoE, if only a global density constraint is applied, the model \"decides\" how to allocate its computational budget across layers.\n\n![Emergent expert activation density across layers. The model naturally learns a three-stage structure: high activation in early layers, low variance in middle layers, and high specialization/variance in late layers.](https://paper-assets.alphaxiv.org/figures-normalized/figures/2604.00801v1/x11.png)_Figure 4: Emergent expert activation density across layers. The model naturally learns a three-stage structure: high activation in early layers, low variance in middle layers, and high specialization/variance in late layers._\n\nThis emergent behavior follows a three-stage progression:\n\n1. **Early Layers:** High activation density to capture broad features.\n2. **Middle Layers:** Low and stable activation density.\n3. **Late Layers:** Highly specialized activation with high variance, where the model selectively engages experts to refine specific outputs.\n\nBy allowing the model to deviate from a uniform sparsity per layer, RFMoE achieved significantly better perplexity (improving from 39.44 to 28.74 at the smallest scale).\n\n## Deployment Efficiency\n\nBeyond architectural benefits, RFMoE offers advantages in distributed deployment. In expert-parallel scenarios, where experts are distributed across multiple GPUs, standard MoE requires complex \"All-to-All\" communication to route tokens to their assigned experts. This centralized step often becomes a sequential bottleneck.\n\nBecause RFMoE eliminates the centralized router, it reduces the complexity of these communication patterns. The authors observed higher throughput in samples per second compared to standard MoE, especially in the autoregressive decoding stage where latency is most critical.\n\nAdditionally, the global threshold θ\\\\thetaθ allows for post-training \"inference-time adaptation.\" By slightly increasing θ\\\\thetaθ, a user can reduce the computational load during inference with a predictable and graceful degradation in model quality. This flexibility is not easily achieved in TopK-based models where the number of experts is hard-coded into the architecture.\n\n## Significance of the Work\n\nThe transition from centralized routing to decentralized, expert-internal activation represents a significant shift in MoE design. By removing the rigid inductive biases of TopK selection and Softmax normalization, RFMoE allows experts to specialize more effectively and provides a more robust training framework.\n\nThe integration of a unified load-balancing loss and an adaptive coefficient schedule simplifies the training process, making large-scale sparse models more accessible and easier to tune. As LLMs continue to scale, the architectural flexibility and emergent efficiency patterns demonstrated by RFMoE provide a potential roadmap for more adaptive and resource-efficient AI systems.\n\nSwitch transformers: Scaling to trillion parameter models with simple and efficient sparsity\n\nThis paper establishes the 'standard MoE' model architecture and training practices that 'Routing-Free MoE' uses as its primary baseline for comparison. The proposed method also extends the auxiliary load-balancing loss function introduced in this work.\n\nWilliam Fedus, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. Switch transformers: Scaling to trillion parameter models with simple and efficient sparsity. Journal of Machine Learning Research, 23(120):1–39.\n\n[Autonomy-of-experts models](https://alphaxiv.org/abs/2501.13074)\n\nThis work is a direct architectural predecessor. The 'Routing-Free MoE' paper explicitly adopts the FFN design from Autonomy-of-Experts (AoE), which generates expert suitability scores from within the expert itself, a core principle of the proposed router-free design.\n\nAng Lv, Ruobing Xie, Yining Qian, Songhao Wu, Xingwu Sun, Zhanhui Kang, Di Wang, and Rui Yan. 2025. Autonomy-of-experts models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2501.13074.\n\nRemoe: Fully differentiable mixture-of-experts with relu routing\n\nThis paper is another key architectural inspiration. The proposed method builds directly on ReMoE's approach of replacing the standard TopK and Softmax functions with a simpler ReLU-based gating mechanism to determine expert activation, which is a central component of the 'Routing-Free' design.\n\nZiteng Wang, Jun Zhu, and Jianfei Chen. 2024b. Remoe: Fully differentiable mixture-of-experts with relu routing. In The Thirteenth International Conference on Learning Representations.\n\nMixture-of-experts with expert choice routing\n\nThis paper introduced Expert Choice (EC) routing, a major load-balancing paradigm that contrasts with the more common Token Choice (TC). The main paper's unified load-balancing framework is designed to explicitly interpolate between and unify these two distinct strategies.\n\nYanqi Zhou, Tao Lei, Hanxiao Liu, Nan Du, Yanping Huang, Vincent Y Zhao, Andrew Dai, Zhifeng Chen, Quoc Le, and James Laudon. 2022. Mixture-of-experts with expert choice routing.
